The 24th presant, beinge Saterdaye, about noune, at the west parte of the subarbes belonginge to this citye, at least a course without the walles, in th’ Allum gange<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-07-fn-037" href="#sec-07-fn-037" data-fn-target="sec-07-fn-037" role="doc-noteref">6</a></sup>, a tirable fier kindled, which <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-301" data-page="301" data-folio="247" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories01fost/page/n300_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 247">[p. 247]</button> havinge consumed al those partes, by the fource of a stronge andye<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-07-fn-038" href="#sec-07-fn-038" data-fn-target="sec-07-fn-038" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup>, brake into the citte and within the space of two greese<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-07-fn-039" href="#sec-07-fn-039" data-fn-target="sec-07-fn-039" role="doc-noteref">2</a></sup> came into the verye harte therof, where our aboade is; whoe beinge enviorned with neighboringe choperes<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-07-fn-040" href="#sec-07-fn-040" data-fn-target="sec-07-fn-040" role="doc-noteref">3</a></sup> (wherof indeede the whole cittye consistes), it was no more then tyme to looke to our owne, which were not many, yet more then in so littell a warninge could bee conveyed of, althoughe I wanted not th’ asistance of almost a hundred of my workmen then at worke. But where the contrary element was wantinge, it was littele bote to contend with the furye of thother; and therfor gave waye to its voyolence of fourse, to save that which most requiered ayde in this nesesitye, which was the mayne of our maisteres goods then under charge, which by good helpe I conveyed by a back waye into a stone house neare adjoyninge. But before yt was entirlye efected, a choper before my chamber toke fyer, and in an instant was consumed, as also the chamber itselfe and all that therin was, save my accompts and monyes, which with as much dificultye as dainger I atayned; of ought elce not anye signe lefte of what it was; wherof belonginge to the Companye in a chest was theise pertickuleres: the remaynder of the bone lace, 16 peeces; the amell<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-07-fn-041" href="#sec-07-fn-041" data-fn-target="sec-07-fn-041" role="doc-noteref">4</a></sup>, safron and one peece mohere, with some verouerds [<em>see</em> p. 198] of silke taken, and other trifells standinge in the tankes, which, with all that was once myne and the litell houshould stufe wee had, was entierlye lost. The rest throughe Gods providence had an unexpected deliverance. From hence it proseeded estward unto the verye scirtes of the towne, where, wantinge more combustable matter to mayntayne it selfe, was constreyned to stinke and goe out, havinge lefte behinde litell save ruines of olde walles, ettc. The infinite losses of all men by this disaster are almost incredible to bee reported, besides men, woemen, and children registerde satties<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-07-fn-042" href="#sec-07-fn-042" data-fn-target="sec-07-fn-042" role="doc-noteref">5</a></sup>, upwards of three hundred. And so much lett sufize for relation herof.’ On the 28th they received from Agra further bills for Rs. 5,000, stated to be written at 40 days ‘bandye mudet’ [<em>see</em> p. 236 <em>n.</em>], but really made out at 45. Two of the bills were at once accepted, but the third was at first <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-302" data-page="302" data-folio="248" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories01fost/page/n301_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 248">[p. 248]</button> rejected, the shroffs saying it was ‘not by theire shawe<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-07-fn-043" href="#sec-07-fn-043" data-fn-target="sec-07-fn-043" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup>, but by one Calyane [Kalyān] of Agra’, whose ‘gamoshtye<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-07-fn-044" href="#sec-07-fn-044" data-fn-target="sec-07-fn-044" role="doc-noteref">2</a></sup>’ has now left Patna. They have, however, since accepted the bill, whereupon Hughes, ‘to avoyde sutter jegres<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-07-fn-045" href="#sec-07-fn-045" data-fn-target="sec-07-fn-045" role="doc-noteref">3</a></sup>,’ discounted it. The shroffs intend to ‘natcare<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-07-fn-046" href="#sec-07-fn-046" data-fn-target="sec-07-fn-046" role="doc-noteref">4</a></sup>’ the bill if necessary, and ‘use our cusmonna [<em>see p. 253 n.</em>] for recoverye of the debte there from the said Calyane’. If future bills were made out ‘at twise sevene dayes berbust<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-07-fn-047" href="#sec-07-fn-047" data-fn-target="sec-07-fn-047" role="doc-noteref">5</a></sup>,’ ‘theire would bee much save in the deheig<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-07-fn-048" href="#sec-07-fn-048" data-fn-target="sec-07-fn-048" role="doc-noteref">6</a></sup>,’ especially if they employed as speedy a ‘cassad’ [<em>see p. 145 n.</em>] as the last, who came in eleven days. ‘The exchange hence to Agra is at present but 1¼ rup[ees] per cent. lose betwene the tasye sickaw<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-07-fn-049" href="#sec-07-fn-049" data-fn-target="sec-07-fn-049" role="doc-noteref">7</a></sup> and the hondye [<em>see p. 85 n.</em>] rupeye’; so it would be more profitable to draw on Agra than to await bills from thence. (<em>Copy.</em> 1¾ <em>pp.</em>)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2">ROBERT HUGHES AT PATNA TO THE PRESIDENT AND COUNCIL AT SURAT, MARCH 31, 1621 (<em>Ibid.</em>, vol. i. p. 26).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Money received from Agra and invested. Accounts cleared with Muqarrab Khān. ‘Fifteene dayes since hee departed this cittye and is gon to courte, and Prince Perviz dalye expected here, whose servants hathe alredy umull’ [<em>see p. 236 n.</em>]. Description of the recent fire, which ‘in lese then foure houres consumed almost all the towne’. Receipt of further funds from Agra on the 28th. (<em>Copy.</em> 1¼ <em>pp.</em>)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2">THOMAS KERRIDGE, AT SEA<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-07-fn-050" href="#sec-07-fn-050" data-fn-target="sec-07-fn-050" role="doc-noteref">8</a></sup>, TO PRESIDENT FURSLAND AT BATAVIA, APRIL 9, 1621 (<em>O. C.</em> 950).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Congratulates him on his ‘advancement in India’. ‘God, in the behalfe of my imployers, hathe bine pleased to visite the Company <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-303" data-page="303" data-folio="249" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories01fost/page/n302_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 249">[p. 249]</button> by losse of theire Agra caphilo, not knowen yet whether for a punishement to them, or judgment to those that did it, whose warrant of seckrytie (never before failed) drew it into theire power. And because I was prepared home before this misfortune hapened, I purpose to be an actor in the recovery, if God be pleased to deliver them into our handes; which in regard of the lateness of the season, some do distrust we shall not recover Mocha, in which case we have onely Mesulapatnam to winter in, whence the <em>Hart</em> and <em>Roebuck</em> are to proceed for England, and the <em>London</em> with a prize pinace to returne for Surat, to prosecute the Counsells further directione, whoe I thinke will not give it over untill they have reseved full satisefactione. But if we recover the Red Sea, then dothe our whole fleete returne unto Suratt, and the homeward bound shippes to proceed thence in October in their voyage for England, which God in mercy make prosperous.’ Recommends to his favour Thomas Taylor, the master of the <em>Eagle</em>. Commendations to [Thomas] Brockedon. (<em>Copy</em>. 1 <em>p.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">Thomas Kerridge, aboard the</span> <em>HART</em>, <span class="sc">to the Company</span>, <span class="sc">April</span> 10, 1621 (<em>Abstract only. Factory Records, Miscellaneous</em>, vol. i. p. 108).<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-07-fn-051" href="#sec-07-fn-051" data-fn-target="sec-07-fn-051" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup></h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">‘139,000 mam[ūdīs] in goods sent to the Southwards in the <em>Charles</em>. All the fine goods sould. Our corrall found such opposition that wee were inforced to give the Governor 1,100 r[ial]s of eight, and 2,000 mam[ūdīs] more in the under price of lead. Twenty-eight chests of corrall sould to good profitt. Upon the foresaid bribe wee obtained proclamation for our free trade.’ Hughes sent to Patna, and Rastell to Ahmadābād. ‘Giles James accomptant in Suratt. Edward Haines and Robert Hutchinson sent with goods to Brampore.’ Details of sales. ‘Wee tooke up all the monies wee could at intrest: 106,000 ma[hmūdīs] at Suratt and 50,000 in other places.’ Disposal of money received, &amp;c. Are surprised that the Company have confirmed Salbank for ‘the Red Seas imployment’, as before they judged him unfit. The <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-304" data-page="304" data-folio="250" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories01fost/page/n303_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 250">[p. 250]</button> order in the sea commissions restraining the ships from extraordinary expense of fresh victuals at Surat should be continued. It is understood that the Company wish the factors to refuse in future to supply money for such superfluous expenditure. It is necessary to instruct the commanders not to allow the seamen to come up to Surat; 'wee cannot reforme them.' The Ahmadābād factors blamed for not sending down their accounts in good time. 'Your authoritie given us here to dispose of your shipping is observed, and found your commaunders conformable.' They deny that they entertain idle persons or send away those appointed by the Company to this factory. John Leachland is ordered home. Pike was entertained because of Leachland's sickness. As to a missing parcel of cochineal, and lack of weight in ivory and lead. 250 maunds of the latter deficient. 'Your commission for the future shalbe observed in the receiveing, &amp;c., of all goods by number and waight.' No recovery yet made for the fourteen chests of indigo lost, but they are doing their best to obtain satisfaction. 'Manie pettie rebellions in this countrye since.' They have communicated to Agra and Ahmadābād the Company's censure for not sending home the particular weight of each bale of indigo. 'The newe indico doth much decrease in weight.' 'The reason of the falling and riseing of indico.' For 'provisions of your lynen' they have done their best and have made trial in divers places. The Company must advise them which sorts they prefer. Care will be taken at Ahmadābād in packing and receiving indigo. Twenty-eight fardles have been sent home 'of a new packing' for trial. They will be careful to see that no dirt or stones be packed in the fardles. 'It would be prejudiciall to dissolve your factorie at Amadavaz.' In future they will use half a maund of the best wool in packing each bale of calico. They will discontinue the supply of 'eranees' [<em>see</em> p. 62] and coloured baftas, except crimsons and carnations. They hope to send 5,000 pieces of 'watchett baftas'. Four pieces of 'dimittees<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-07-fn-052" href="#sec-07-fn-052" data-fn-target="sec-07-fn-052" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup>' sent. 'You shall have no more long nor extraordinary broad baftees.' No turmeric to be got at present. Supply of gum-lac, carpets, sealing-wax, Bengalia silk, quilts, conserves, &amp;c. 'Mattes of <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-305" data-page="305" data-folio="251" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories01fost/page/n304_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 251">[p. 251]</button> canes provided for dennedge<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-07-fn-053" href="#sec-07-fn-053" data-fn-target="sec-07-fn-053" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup>.’ ‘The number from henceforth upon your callicoes shalbe the prices they cost.’ The Agra factors will enter the number, colour, and length of each broadcloth sold, and the Ahmadābād factors will note all bargains plainly ‘and what sand they putt into each bale’. Death of John Browne, and disposal of his estate. ‘Thomas Kerridge retourneth and Thomas Rastell succeedeth him in place.’ ‘William Biddulph will retourne. Hee hath compounded for the great court debt long owinge. Robert Hewes to succeed William Biddulph in the courts agentshipp. Staying of the Surat junck must be attempted. Redd Sea busines thought upon. Noe tradinge for conveniency att Dabull. Wee advertised the Guzeratts of the expectation or doubt of Danes, French, and Dutch in the Redd Sea, &amp;c. Articles of the Union<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-07-fn-054" href="#sec-07-fn-054" data-fn-target="sec-07-fn-054" role="doc-noteref">2</a></sup> receaved. Your servants noe doubt will hold them, but wee doubt how their[s?] will keepe them. Noe saltpeeter about Suratt.<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-07-fn-055" href="#sec-07-fn-055" data-fn-target="sec-07-fn-055" role="doc-noteref">3</a></sup> Corasan silke. Supplie for Persia against the next yeare wilbe made.’ Sale of their English commodities. ‘A resoluccion of stoppinge the Princes juncke for procuringe libertye to land our corrall and redressing some other wrongs. The jewells and quicksilver sent to the courte. Diamonds or other jewells besett with diamonds are lesse esteemed then formerly.’ ‘Our rials are not suffered to be exported.’ ‘Some investments wee will make privately for Mocha against the coming of the next shipps.’ The Company’s injunctions for investing 40,000 rials for Sumatra and Java noted. ‘Your retournes for England are allwaies first provided, and then for other places according to your meanes left. According to the largnes of your stock in this countrie you maie expect your retournes. The next yeare you maie expect large retournes. Your commissions given for defence and offence of the Portugalls seeme to bee too weake, considering their open hostillity against you. They have noe inclynation to anie peace except it bee made betweene both the Kings Majesties.’ ‘Edward Haynes <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-306" data-page="306" data-folio="252" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories01fost/page/n305_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 252">[p. 252]</button> could not bee called from Brampore to goe for the Red Sea this winter. John Goodman [Goodwin] and Henry Edmonds have had notice of the augmentation of their wages.’ They recommend William Martin to the Company’s consideration. Recovery of an old debt. ‘Henry Darrell went for Persia. Justinian Offley appointed for Brodera; John Clarke for Amadavaz. Sir Thomas Roes tokens unto the Jesuite [<em>see</em> p. 15] and Dutchman<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-07-fn-056" href="#sec-07-fn-056" data-fn-target="sec-07-fn-056" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> shalbe delivered.’ ‘Most necessarie to restraine your ship masters from vaine expence of powder.<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-07-fn-057" href="#sec-07-fn-057" data-fn-target="sec-07-fn-057" role="doc-noteref">2</a></sup> Your factors desire noe such enterteynments and therefore desire to be freed. Two chirurgions enterteyned in Suratt, the one whereof wee will send for Persia to acquite the charge of the Scottish phisition’ [<em>see</em> p. 237]. The commanders should be ordered to facilitate the lading of the ships destined for England. Damage to certain bales. ‘Walter Harveye dead.’ Wills sent home. ‘A Germaine enterteined into the factorie, pretended to bee sent out by the Companie. [“But I knowe noe such matter.”—<em>Note by the abstractor</em>.] Needfull to give your President of Suratt full power over all other your depending factories; and that the President also should sett lymitts unto the Agent at court, seeing the best waie to remedy your wroungs of this people is in your President at Suratt to staie their juncks. Requisite to have a Counsell at Suratt of four sufficient men and a Register constantly resident. One experienced merchant (besides your President and Accomptant) for manie necessarie purposes. Factors to be experienced men. Advise concerninge the Redd Sea trade. The Portugalls have noe great trade in Dabull, yet keepe a factorie there to assist their merchaunts that come, and give licence to the Mores navigation. The proffitt by the vent of our English commodities would defraie the charge of a factorye att Dabull. After Dabull hath made satisfaction for our caphila, wee maie make triall of a factorie there. Batacala [<em>see</em> p. 56 <em>n.</em>] noe doubt yeildeth store of pepper, but the power of the Portugalls restrayneth the sale of them unto the Kinge of Spaines subjects onely. The <em>Eagle</em> departed to the Southwards.’ (4½ <em>pp.</em>)</p></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-307"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="307" data-folio="253" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories01fost/page/n306_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 253">[p. 253]</button></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">Robert Hughes at Patna to the Factors at Agra, April</span> 11, 1621 (<em>Factory Records, Patna</em>, vol. i. p. 27).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The bills for Rs. 3,000 have been duly received and accepted. Inquires the prices of raw silk in Agra. The serbandy has fallen ‘a savoye’ [<em>see</em> p. 194] in price; so they can produce the raw silk much cheaper. ‘Wee have ventured 500 rup[ees] to Mucksoudabaude [<em>see</em> p. 194] for samples [of] silke of the sortes wee provide, rather for experience of that place then the necesitye therof, beinge encouradged therunto by good liklyehood of principall commoditye and at much easier rates then theise partes afordes. The voyadge is but two monthes.’ ‘Sultan Parvez is shortlye expected here, and yf you intende a setled imployment, it would not bee enconvenient that you sent us somthinge wherwith wee might make causmana<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-07-fn-058" href="#sec-07-fn-058" data-fn-target="sec-07-fn-058" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> with him.’ (<em>Copy.</em> 1 <em>p.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">Agreement between the English and Dutch Presidencies at Batavia for regulating the Joint Trade at Pulicat, April</span> 13, 1621 (<em>Treaties</em>, vol. i. p. 109).<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-07-fn-059" href="#sec-07-fn-059" data-fn-target="sec-07-fn-059" role="doc-noteref">2</a></sup></h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">I. The Dutch authorities will either provide the English factors with a house (charging a reasonable rent for the same) or allot a site on which they may build for themselves, and in the meanwhile will find them convenient lodgings. II. The English merchants will inform the Dutch what sorts and quantities of cloth they desire to buy. If the latter intend to purchase any of the same kind, the goods will be bought jointly at a price agreed upon and afterwards divided. III. The English share of the charges and maintenance of the fort and the wages of the garrison is fixed by the seventh article of the Treaty of Defence. If they cannot furnish the victuals or warlike munition required they will pay for any brought by the Dutch from Batavia at the rate fixed upon for the Moluccas, Amboyna, and Banda. Provisions bought in the <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-308" data-page="308" data-folio="254" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories01fost/page/n307_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 254">[p. 254]</button> country will be charged at cost price. IV. Damages to the fort, its ordnance, provisions, &amp;c., will be repaired at the joint expense. V. The wages and maintenance of the Dutch merchants ' and other inferior persons' shall not be charged as garrison expenses. VI. The servants of both Companies are expressly prohibited from private trade in cloth, under penalty of confiscation of the goods and further punishment. VII. Although these common charges might strictly be held to commence from the publication of the Treaty, the Dutch have agreed to waive for the present any claim for expenditure anterior to the arrival of the English at Pulicat. VIII. The Dutch will render to the English at Pulicat a monthly account of the charges for the garrisons, &amp;c., and the latter will thereupon pay their share to the Dutch Governor. (<em>Copy. Carpentier and Dedel sign for the Dutch, and Fursland and Brockedon for the English.</em> 11 <em>pp.</em>)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">William Methwold at Masulipatam to the Company</span>, <span class="sc">May</span> 12, 1621 (<em>Abstract only. Factory Records, Miscellaneous</em>, vol. i. p. 84).<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-07-fn-060" href="#sec-07-fn-060" data-fn-target="sec-07-fn-060" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup></h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Complains that, after serving them for nearly five years, they have listened to false accusations and called him home without hearing his defence. Diamonds bought and sent home in charge of George Ball. He wrote on January 23 by way of Surat [<em>not extant</em>]. Some of the goods ordered by the Company from Surat might be furnished from Masulipatam. There is store of gum-lac to be had at the latter place, of two sorts, and at cheaper rates than formerly. 'Of Musilpatann callicoes I sent musters to Surrat to be sent for England. The Dutch ship <em>Medenblick</em> here is most laded with long callicoes, whereof I have bought 200 peeces to <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-309" data-page="309" data-folio="255" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories01fost/page/n308_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 255">[p. 255]</button> be sent with the first for England for a triall. The Dutch have bought Corromandell indico at 30 pago[das] the 12½ maunes, ech maun 26 seers. I made a journey (with the Dutch Governor) to the diamond myne<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-07-fn-061" href="#sec-07-fn-061" data-fn-target="sec-07-fn-061" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup>, and retorned in twelve dayes, with the expence of ten pagodas. The blackes there admired our habitts and complexions. We sawe aboundance, but bought none. We left ech of us a man behinde us with 200 r[ials] a peece to bestowe after we were gone, for they thought we brought aboundance of monie to buy, and therefore helde them deare. Our men, having bestowed their monie in dimons and comyng home, were robd and so lost them all [<em>see</em> p. 217]. I have bought since at Musilpatam diamondes for 1,419 pagodes for this account, and for 534 p[agodes] for the account of the retornes from Pegu [<em>see</em> p. 209]. Theis I would have sent in this Dutch ship if I had had anie warrant thereunto. The Dutch have bought good store. I have also bought some bezar stones, some for 24 r[ial]s per sere (which is 12 oz.), and some derer. James Cartwright, second to William Methwold, died 6 September, 1620.’ Disposal of his estate. William Smith died April 29, 1621. News from Surat regarding the disposal of the fleet, &amp;c. ‘Our ships made staie of two Surrat junckes, but dismissed them againe after they had supplyed ours with powder and shott.’ ‘I received a letter from Syam, whereby I finde that Musilpatan goods make two for one in Siam. The factors at Jaquatra neglect to supply Syam.’ ‘The English ship <em>Beare</em> and the Dutch <em>Medemblicke</em> arrived at Musilpatan for the Dutch. They could procure no pepper at Achin under 64 rials the bahar.’ News from Achin and Jakatra. ‘A Frensh ship having in the Red Sea sped not well, she came to Achin also for pepper and procureth trade there, but lyeth long and performeth little. Hir men die apace. A Frensh factor cometh to Musilpatan to make preparacion for trade against the coming of their ship. A report that the Dutch have surprized the other Frensh neere Jaquatra<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-07-fn-062" href="#sec-07-fn-062" data-fn-target="sec-07-fn-062" role="doc-noteref">2</a></sup>. A relacion of the Danes ships and their plantacions. The Dutch did chace a frigat of the Danes, thincking them to <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-310" data-page="310" data-folio="256" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories01fost/page/n309_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 256">[p. 256]</button> be Portugales, &amp;c.<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-07-fn-063" href="#sec-07-fn-063" data-fn-target="sec-07-fn-063" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> I expect my Nunc Dimittis, and hope to come in the ferst ship, according to your absolute command of <em>Send him home.</em>' (1 <sup>1</sup>⁄<sub>2</sub> <em>pp.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">Robert Hughes at Patna to the Factors at Agra</span>, <span class="sc">May</span> 19, 1621 (<em>Factory Records, Patna</em>, vol. i. p. 28).<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-07-fn-064" href="#sec-07-fn-064" data-fn-target="sec-07-fn-064" role="doc-noteref">2</a></sup></h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Bills for Rs. 8,000 received; also the list of goods required for Surat. What goods they had already provided were this morning laden on two carts for Agra, 'and have made theire first manzull' [<em>see</em> p. 74 <em>n.</em>]. They have agreed to pay 1 <sup>3</sup>⁄<sub>4</sub> rupees 1 'tuke<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-07-fn-065" href="#sec-07-fn-065" data-fn-target="sec-07-fn-065" role="doc-noteref">3</a></sup>' per maund net, which is the current rate of the caravan they go with. A native has been sent in charge, with six 'tierandazes' [<em>see</em> p. 74 <em>n.</em>] for a guard. They have sent all their coarse silk, as no purchaser could be found at Patna. Some fine goods would be useful for presents. (<em>Copy.</em> 1 <em>p.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">The Same to the Same, June</span> 2, 1621 (<em>Ibid.</em>, vol. i. p. 29).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Acknowledges the receipt of bills for a further sum of Rs. 8,000. He is very sorry to hear of the death of Fettiplace. 'Wee apprehend Surrats resolution for the desolvinge this factory (our this yeres provisions beinge accomplished).' Hughes cannot repair to Agra, as desired, until the end of the rains, owing to 'the heate of our present busines and Mr. Parkers indisposition of helthe'. 'The Princes<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-07-fn-066" href="#sec-07-fn-066" data-fn-target="sec-07-fn-066" role="doc-noteref">4</a></sup> arivall here with so greate a retienewe hath made this place to narowe for his entertaynment, which hathe caused the removinge diverse, as well merchants as otheres, from theire aboades, whose houses hee hath liberalye bestowed on his servants; amongst which couppelment wee are displaced, and have bine theise ten dayes wandringe to cover ourselves and goods, thoughe but with grase, to debar the heate and raynes, now in excesse; which havinge now attayned throughe the helpe of Mr. Monye<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-07-fn-067" href="#sec-07-fn-067" data-fn-target="sec-07-fn-067" role="doc-noteref">5</a></sup>, wee endevor agayne the plasinge our silkwynders.' (<em>Copy.</em> 1 <sup>1</sup>⁄<sub>4</sub> <em>pp.</em>)</p></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-311"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="311" data-folio="257" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories01fost/page/n310_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 257">[p. 257]</button></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">The Same to the President and Council at Surat, June</span> 2, 1621 (<em>Ibid.</em>, vol. i. p. 30).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">They have noted the order to dissolve this factory at the end of the season and return to Agra, where Hughes is to succeed Fettiplace. He cannot, however, leave Patna until the recovery of Parker, ‘whoe almost theise three monthes hathe layne daingerouslye sicke of the blodye fluxe.’ They have now received from Agra in all Rs. 29,000 for this year’s investments, in addition to money obtained by the sale of goods. They propose to draw for a further Rs. 10,000, as they hear that Young has little hope of procuring half the narrow cloth expected from him, and so they must endeavour to provide the balance at Patna. Seventeen bales of silk and calicoes were dispatched to Agra on May 18. (<em>Copy.</em> 1 <em>p.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">William Biddulph at Agra to the Factors at Ispahān, June</span> 15, 1621 (<em>Factory Records, Persia</em>, vol. i. p. 3).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">They have no doubt heard from Surat that the fleet returned in safety from Persia in February last. Two of the ships were fully laden for England, but, their sailing having been deferred until too late, the whole fleet has now gone to Mokha to winter, and President Kerridge with them. Last March ‘the Dechanes, beinge in warrs with this Kinge’, seized an English ‘caffyla’ of 350 camels, carrying ‘the whole investments the last yeare in this place, Puttana and Semiana’; but it is hoped that ‘the Dabullers wilbe forced to give satysfaction to the utmost farthinge’. ‘Some twoo months passed heere arrived some nine or ten Dutch to setle a factorye heere, who brought with them certeyne comodities.’ Francis Fettiplace died of a fever on May 15. The bearers are ‘two gentlemen, Venetians, who are not unknowne to you’. ‘They are my auntient accquaintance . . and have shewed much love to our nacion to theyr powers.’ Begs that they may be well treated. At the same time he requests ‘a recyprocall and mutuall correspondence’ between the two factories. (<em>Copy.</em> 1¼ <em>pp.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">Robert Young at ‘Semeane’ to the President and Council at Surat, August</span> 2, 1621 (<em>Brit. Mus. Egerton MS.</em> 2123, <em>f.</em> 47).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Has bought upwards of 2,000 pieces of narrow calicoes, and hopes to get 5,000 in all by the time of his departure, which will probably <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-312" data-page="312" data-folio="258" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories01fost/page/n311_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 258">[p. 258]</button> be about September 25, in order to reach Agra by the middle of October. He thinks it unnecessary to have a permanent factory here, as 'semmeanes' can be bought as cheaply at Agra, charges considered. As regards Patna, the factors there will know best, but he holds it a more fitting place than Samāna, as it is reported to 'affoorde greater qua[ntities] of well-made cloth, which is more fitting our co[untry] then the cloth of these parts, being for the m[ost part] of a slighter makeing and of uncertayne len[gths] and breadths'. (<em>A much mutilated copy.</em> 1½ <em>pp.</em>)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">Robert Hughes at 'Lackhoare' to the President and Council at Surat, August</span> 3, 1621 (<em>Factory Records, Patna</em>, vol. i. p. 31).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">It is impossible to send any more goods until October, 'here beinge noe other convayance to Agra but by carts, which by reason of the rottenes of the wayes in the season of the raynes passeth nott.' They have up to the present bought 9,500 pieces of calico, more than two-thirds 'at or under two rup[ees] nett the piece, as bought browne from the loome, and are the desired breadthes, to say, the second sorte generally knowne by the name of Jafferchanes [<em>see</em> p. 193], which both for length and breadth will parallel, if nott exceed, your narrowe Barroch baftaes. The remaynder are fyner, broader, and hyer pryzed, to say, from all pryzes from two to six rup[ees] the peece.' In view of their recall, they have ceased from buying silk; 'neither have we mett with any more sahans.' Parker is at Patna buying lignum aloes, of which various sorts are to be had at from two to ten rupees the seer of 33 pices nett. Gum-lac can be purchased for 4½ rupees the maund, but, freight considered, would probably be bought cheaper in Agra. Their amber beads are not worth more than eight or nine rupees the sere of 14 pices. For the price of coral refers them to Parker. (<em>Copy.</em> 1½ <em>pp.</em>)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">John Parker at Patna to the Same, August</span> 7, 1621 (<em>Ibid.</em>, vol. i. p. 32).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">'Of the price and esteeme of currall in these parts . . . I have enquyred of the merchants which deales most in that commodity, who, as they say, never saw unpollished currall brought into these <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-313" data-page="313" data-folio="259" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories01fost/page/n312_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 259">[p. 259]</button> parts, which, if I mistake nott, ys the sort you seeke vent for. In other places yt ys much spent to burne with the dead; which here they use nott. And for pollishinge or cuttinge yt into beads, heer are nott workmen that hath skill therein; and therefore noe commodity for this place. Pollished currall will sell here, but in small quantity; and at what pryce I cannott informe you, the quallity thereof beeing soe different. Currall beads ys very well requested for transporte into Bengala, and great quantityes thereof will yearly vend, to say for 50 or 60,000 rup[ees], at or about the pryzes followinge, viz. those of 12 beads to a tanke<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-07-fn-068" href="#sec-07-fn-068" data-fn-target="sec-07-fn-068" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> at 6 tanks for a rup[ee]: of 6 to a tanke at 2 tanks for a rup[ee]: of 4 to a tanke at 1¾ tanks per rup[ee]: of 3 to a tanke at 1½ per rup[ee]: and soe accordinge to theire bignes. For the sale of our remaynder of amber beads . . . I have shewed them to dyvers merchants since Mr. Hughes his beeing at Lackhoare, butt cannott attayne to above 9 rup[ees] the sere, at which rate rather then retourne them for Agra would putt them off, butt yt ys nott a commodity which yeilds ready mony, and by reason of our sudden departure I dare nott trust them out, though should be promysed payment within ten daies. For the future sendinge of which commodity I cannott anymate you, yt beeing a commodity that will nott sell in any great quantity, but in small parcells, as for 100 and 150 rup[ees], which will nott goe far therein; soe a small quantity will furnish a great many of these merchants. I have nott yett provyded the gum-lacke, nor elce for musters, the merchants, brokers, shop-keepers ettc. of the citty beeinge all in trouble for mony which the Prince requyers them to furnish him with; soe that none dares be seene to sell a pyce worth of goods. But now he beeinge gone, I shall soone imploy the small matter intended therein.’ (<em>Copy.</em> 1 <em>p.</em>)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">Robert Hughes and John Parker at Patna to the Company, August</span> 14, 1621 (<em>Ibid.</em>, vol. i. p. 33).<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-07-fn-069" href="#sec-07-fn-069" data-fn-target="sec-07-fn-069" role="doc-noteref">2</a></sup></h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The goods provided last year were, on their way to Surat, ‘robed and spoyled by the Decans armye.’ The President and Council <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-314" data-page="314" data-folio="260" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories01fost/page/n313_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 260">[p. 260]</button> promised an early supply of money for a fresh investment, but it was March before any remittance was received. In all, Rs. 32,000 have been sent them, and the sale of their goods brought in Rs. 4,000 more. They were directed to purchase 100 maunds of Bengal silk and 20,000 pieces of ‘amberty callicoes of Lackhoare’; but their funds will not suffice for such a quantity, and Surat can spare no more money. They hope, however, to get together 10,000 pieces. Of sahan cloth they have only been able to procure twelve ‘corges’, costing 78 rupees net. Of silk the supply will not exceed 25 maunds. They hope to leave Patna with their goods about the end of September, and will send full particulars from Agra. (<em>Copy. 2 pp.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">William Biddulph and John Young at Agra to the President and Council at Surat, August</span> 22, 1621 (<em>Brit. Mus. Egerton MS. 2123, f.</em> 54).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Have not been able to procure all the proofs they needed in the matter of ‘Herpa[xad’s]’ [Hariprasād’s] debt; so they must leave the prosecution of it to Surat. Nor can they yet supply the account requested of their cattle. ‘Wee perceave if againe you attempt the stayeing the Princes junck you will execute it to more resolute purpose; desiring it have noe worse success then the former, for at that time it procured your redresse for your then present occasion, with little or noe molestation to the Honnorable Company [       ].’ ‘Concerning the exporting of your ryalls and desireing to land them at Goga [<em>see</em> p. 29] with the assistance of Asufchans perwanna<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-07-fn-070" href="#sec-07-fn-070" data-fn-target="sec-07-fn-070" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup>, wee have bin something large therein in our former; the Nababob [<em>sic</em>]<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-07-fn-071" href="#sec-07-fn-071" data-fn-target="sec-07-fn-071" role="doc-noteref">2</a></sup> absolutely refuseing to give any wryting to that purpose, the Prince being soe neere and himself soe farr of, and that it will but breed us more trouble and discontent to offer to land parte of our goods at Surratt and parte in other ports that is not in his custodye. Soe that heere is noe relefe for that busines to be expected from hence, but must use meanes to the Prince himselfe for redresse of such injuries, which if shall denye, or not give eare unto it, the stopping of his shipp will force [       ].’ As to the provision and dispatch of goods. Indigo will be dear, ‘such unaccustomed raynes . . . hath drowned the greatest parte of new indicoe in the countryes.’ They will observe the orders as <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-315" data-page="315" data-folio="261" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories01fost/page/n314_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 261">[p. 261]</button> to the placing of factors, and have recalled Hughes from Patna, leaving Parker in charge there until Young arrives. It will be necessary that Biddulph should attend the King, especially if the Prince's junk is to be stopped, when explanations are sure to be demanded. ‘At present it is uncertaine whether hee make his progresse for Adsmeere, Mandoo, or Lahore.’ For the differences with the customs officials, they can procure no certificate in their favour, ‘and Asufchan will give noe wryting at all to the Princes people. The Kings officers denye to give any wryting, the prices of our comoditie being made per the Prince and his officers in Jadoe Rayes [Jādū Rāy's] duffters<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-07-fn-072" href="#sec-07-fn-072" data-fn-target="sec-07-fn-072" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup>, whoe was then Mere Saman<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-07-fn-073" href="#sec-07-fn-073" data-fn-target="sec-07-fn-073" role="doc-noteref">2</a></sup> for the Prince, hath all the severall prices written, whoe cannot denye the certifieing the truth of the severall rates.’ Force the best remedy. Fettiplace's estate. Some of the quicksilver has been sold, but the ivory is still on hand. (<em>Copy. Damaged.</em> 4 <em>pp.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">Matthew Duke at Masulipatam to the Company</span>, <span class="sc">August</span> 27, 1621 (<em>O. C.</em> 983).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">‘Your ship the <em>Globe</em> (after sum few daies staie at Pelicat and ther landing her merchants, etc.) arived here in the road of Mesulepatnam the 15th June, passing by Petapolie without staie, which, yf [<em>sic</em>], might have prevented a great deale of troble and much danger, for at foure or five daies warning wee could have ladne 120 fardles of goods without troble or danger; but it so chanced that the said ship staying longe at Mesulepatnam at last at unseasonable time made tryall to com for Petapolie, which, had that tryall ben but one daie sooner, th’ atempt had ben effected to content, but missing the good opertunety, after eight or ten daies tryall and not able to obteine the port, returned to Mesulepatnam. It was then high time to think on sum other corse how to get our goods to Mesulepatnam, and the 5th curant I had absolute order to provid boat or boats for transportacion of your goods in Petapolie to Mesulepatnam. The 7th ditto I imbarqued all your said goods, 158 fardles, besides much other provisons and lumber, being made beleeve that the next daie wee should passe the bar and set saile for Mesulepatnam; but it was the 12th before wee atempted it, when erly in the morning, the wind and tide serving, wee set saille. But <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-316" data-page="316" data-folio="262" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories01fost/page/n315_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 262">[p. 262]</button> being but entred the bar, the wind turnes contrary, the tide being then against us to returne, were forced to com to anker, but the suffe of the seaes caried us violently on the shoule, where wee laie beating the space of one houre without hoope of being saved. But loe in time of moast extremety (when helpe of man could not prevaill) the mightie King of heaven and earth, our good and gratious God, did so provide for our saffety that a little asterne the barque there was deep water into the which the barque was beatne by the waves, and soe mersefully saved from wrecke. Gods holy and blissed name be praysed therefore. Haveing escaped this great danger, I presently caused beter cables and ankers to be provided; which being fitted, the nex daie with helpe of four boats wee passed the bar, and the 14th came salfely to the <em>Globe</em> in the road of Mesulepatnam. But it being sunset before our arivall, could not take in our goods that night, whan at nine of the clocke in the night fell a terible storme of raine with much wind, and wett fourteen of the fardles, so that they were fain to be taken on shoar and dryed.’ Some of the bundles got wet again in being carried back to the ship, but the damage is slight. ‘The ship the <em>Globe</em> set saille emediatly after my departure, and yesterdaie, the 26 curent, before I got on shore was quit out of sight. I pray God prosper her and send her a good and speedy passage to Jackatra. It is thougth good by Mr. Methwold to desolve the factory of Petapolie, for saving of charges; but yf I might have perswaded, Petapolie should have yet continued for one year, till better experience made of Policat, for divers reasons to longe here to incert; but in this little time I have been here in Mesulepatnam I well perceive cloath is dearer then in Petapolie by more then ten per cent. At my leaving Petapolie I left in debts owing to that account only one of 32½ pa[godas], and sould the night I came awaie, at midnight or near that time, all the remainer of our brasse, vizt. 5 ca[ndes], 13 ma[nes], 1 veis<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-07-fn-074" href="#sec-07-fn-074" data-fn-target="sec-07-fn-074" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup>, sould at 60 pag[odas] new per cande, containing 20 ma[nes]; each mane I doe estem at 26 lb. haberdepoise, and the new pagoda worth about 1½ riall of eight, Spanish money. Also I sould ditto time 36 ca[ndes] of lead at 14½ pa[godas] old per conde, to pay at three monthes. Wee did formerly sell small quanteties at 20, 18, 17, and 16 pa[godas] per cande, till latly <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-317" data-page="317" data-folio="263" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories01fost/page/n316_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 263">[p. 263]</button> inordred per Mr. Methwold I sould at last prise mentioned near 60 cand[es]; and had I stayed but four daies more I thinke I should have sould all the rest. This was sould to suffitient cometes [<em>see</em> p. 47] to be paid at three monthes time; but coming to Mesulepatnam Mr. Methwold would have no more sould at that prise unles they paid redy mony. The purselaine remaines in statu quo prius, which I locked up and brought the keye with me; th' other goods, being only lead, I left in charge with an Englishman named Richard Smith, a sailer (I having first told over all the bars of lead and carefully taken the contents of each bar according to the stampe marked on them); and I left directions with him, naming the parties that hee might trust in the sale therof, and not to sell, unless for redy mony, to eynie other parte. Yet, to ad farther to my trouble, the verie instant of my departure I had all my mony that I had six daies before paid for custome returned mee, and was glad to give six pa[godas] more to part quietly without farther let. After my arivall in Mesulepatnam, which was the 15th curent, I was much hastned for my accounts, being made beleeve the ship should not staie above three or four daies at most; but throw much hast missing a quarter pa[goda] in my ballance (which is not usually found at first tryall), I was inforced to deliver only a perfect invoise, promising by next conveiance (English or Dutch) to send jornall and leadger with a perfect ballance, which yet shall not in eynie point differ or alter from invoise sent, for at shipes beeing here I coulnot obteyne so much helpe as one man to hould my jornall whilst I pricked over my leadger. I have bin tyed to these tedious accounts now four years, and although I have ernestly requested to be freed therof, yet cannot, but am by Mr. Methwold yet againe aponted for accountant and casheer here in Mesulepatnam. The keeping of these accounts would not be very tedious yf they were ordred by denomenacions of moneyes that hath even parts in it, as 8 ca[sh] to the fanam and 16 fan[ams] to a pagodo, which will produce no les fraction then ½ c[ash], but being kept at 9 c[ash] to the fan[am] and 15 fa[nams] to the pagodo breeds intollarable fractions not fitting merchants accounts, for ther is more troble to reduce the fraction then about all the rest of thaccount; and when all is done, not the value of 3<em>d.</em> found more or les. But when these thing[s] shall com to the generall bookekeeper to enter, <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-318" data-page="318" data-folio="264" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories01fost/page/n317_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 264">[p. 264]</button> what a labrinth it will bring him into. But these things would be ordred at home by yourselves that for the future these erors may be avoyded (which I may justly cale erors); and yf I thought I should be tyed to theis intrecat accounts, I would rather serve other waise without wages then with doble wages continue in them, for although I know well enough how to reduce a fraction to his least denomenacion, yet to bee much troubled with them is tedeou[s], and rediculus to keep them in such great account.’ He sends an abstract of the invoice of the goods for Jakatra; also particulars of charges. Provisions purchased for the ship and for the English house at Jakatra. <em>Annexed</em>: Abstract of goods laden on the <em>Globe</em>, amounting to 11,198 pagodas 2 <sup>9</sup>/<sub>16</sub> cash. <em>Also</em>, the following list of charges, viz. :—</p></div><div class="ledger-table-block" id="tbl-005"><div class="table-toolbar"><span class="table-id-badge">TBL-005 · FOLIO 318</span><span class="table-csv-links"><a href="/downloads/english-factories-india-1618-1621-vol-01/datasets/tables/tbl-005_p0318.csv" download>Diplomatic CSV</a> · <a href="/downloads/english-factories-india-1618-1621-vol-01/datasets/tidy/tbl-005_p0318.tidy.csv" download>Tidy CSV</a></span></div><div class="ledger-table-wrap" role="region" aria-label="Statistical table" tabindex="0"><table class="ledger-table"><thead><tr><th scope="col"></th><th scope="col">Pag.</th><th scope="col">fan.</th><th scope="col">cash.</th></tr></thead><tbody><tr class="row-data"><td>Custom and ‘dalalie’<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-07-fn-075" href="#sec-07-fn-075" data-fn-target="sec-07-fn-075" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup></td><td class="num">346.</td><td class="num">0.</td><td class="num">6 <sup>1</sup>/<sub>4</sub></td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td>Gifts (two years)</td><td class="num">56.</td><td class="num">1.</td><td class="num">8 <sup>5</sup>/<sub>8</sub></td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td>Expense of diet (19 months)</td><td class="num">286.</td><td class="num">3.</td><td class="num">5 <sup>1</sup>/<sub>4</sub></td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td>Extraordinaries (ditto)</td><td class="num">383.</td><td class="num">1.</td><td class="num">7 <sup>5</sup>/<sub>16</sub></td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td>Charges of merchandise (ditto)</td><td class="num">200.</td><td class="num">9.</td><td class="num">0 <sup>1</sup>/<sub>4</sub></td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td>Salaries (two years)</td><td class="num">433.</td><td class="num">10.</td><td class="num">0</td></tr><tr class="row-total"><td></td><td class="num">1705.</td><td class="num">12.</td><td class="num">0 <sup>11</sup>/<sub>16</sub></td></tr></tbody></table></div></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">(<em>Holograph.</em> 6 <em>pp.</em>)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2">WILLIAM METHWOLD, MATTHEW DUKE, AND FRANCIS FUTTER AT MASULIPATAM TO THE PRESIDENT AND COUNCIL AT SURAT, AUGUST 27, 1621 (<em>Brit. Mus. Egerton MS. 2123, f. 59</em>).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Apologize for their long silence. The attempt of the <em>Globe</em> to reach Petapoli failed, and they were consequently forced to bring the goods thence in a native boat, with the result that it was only the [      ] present that the <em>Globe</em> sailed. Letters sent ‘per Signor Ravesteine’<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-07-fn-076" href="#sec-07-fn-076" data-fn-target="sec-07-fn-076" role="doc-noteref">2</a></sup>, bound in the <em>Sampson</em> to the Red Sea’. Prices of gum-lac. ‘For silke of Bengala to be procured in this place, I am wholy hopeless to effect, for the trade hether is most uncertaine; some yeares noe marchaunts, other whilest noe passadge; and the last yeare (as I have formerly advised) all taken or stranded by the Portugall.’ They are glad to hear that Surat has gained more <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-319" data-page="319" data-folio="265" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories01fost/page/n318_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 265">[p. 265]</button> freedom by stopping the native junks. It would be well to do something of the kind here to relieve them from oppression. ‘Even of late a new governor, haveing rented this goverment for 15,000 pag[odas] per annum more then his predecessors, hath devised some parte of this excesse per farmeing (?) out the monopoly of one man onely buying, selling, and changing of all gould and silver brought unto this porte; from whence it happens that wee cannot put of our ryalls but as that onely sharoffe [<em>see</em> p. 8 <em>n.</em>] please to take them, which is to more losse then wee have reason to stoope unto . . . but did wee staye their shipps, now ready shortly to departe for Pegu, Tannass[ery], and Achine, wee are perswaded they would shortly resolve to graunt our reasonable requests; and this course the Dutch profess to have, and purpose to put it in practize; onely their conveniency, it seemes, deferrs it untill further tyme.’ Little demand for coral here. They hear that ‘in the kingdom of Adelsha [<em>see</em> p. 54], 10 or 12 dayes jorney from this place, at a citty or towne called Lantegree there resides most of those artificers which are accustomed to pollish corrall, for which they have 10 pag[odas] per maen for their workemanship’. The Dutch import fair round coral beads, ‘whereunto weare annexed great strings or cords to make waight’; but long beads are more in favour. ‘The diamond mine [<em>see</em> pp. 208, 255] is shut up, and diamonds at this present both scarse and deere in this place. . . . It is at Jacatra published per the Dutch that these are not perfect, both weaker and more transparent then those of Succadana<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-07-fn-077" href="#sec-07-fn-077" data-fn-target="sec-07-fn-077" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup>. Notwithstanding, they continew upon all occasion to buy store, and did send by the <em>Medenblique</em> 800 or 1,000 carrete. . . . It is thought the mine will shortly be open againe, and is onely stopt to procure greater rent; which being this yeare raysed’ [<em>torn away</em>]. ‘Pollicat is now stockt both with money and marchaunts, and will in one yeare give proofe of the trade. It is most true you urge how convenient such a retrait weare for our masters affaires upon your coast, the want wherof layes us open to every ones malice and disturbance.’ They wish all success to the fleet going to Mokha. Methwold regrets that the Company is displeased with him. ‘[With ?] the newes of the Portingalls reinforceing [Macao ? c]ame the certainety of the supply sent from <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-320" data-page="320" data-folio="266" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories01fost/page/n319_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 266">[p. 266]</button> Goa to [Ormus?], by the same English surgeons report which escaped from [Macao?] and brought the newes of the lost <em>Unicorne</em>.<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-07-fn-078" href="#sec-07-fn-078" data-fn-target="sec-07-fn-078" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> This coast [is] not yet freed of all the Danes, from whome an Einglish woman, [which] came out in their fleet, a maid about 24, upon a ginga[tha]<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-07-fn-079" href="#sec-07-fn-079" data-fn-target="sec-07-fn-079" role="doc-noteref">2</a></sup> came to Pollecat, and was after little stay there honn[estly] married to the preacher of the fort.’ The [Dutch?] at Masulipatam and Achin. Abstract sent of the cargo of the <em>Globe</em>. They complain that the Company ignore them and have not for many years written to them. (<em>Copy. Much damaged.</em> 8 <em>pp.</em>) <em>Annexed:</em>—Invoice of <em>Globe:</em> ‘Red percallas,’ ‘chowters Bengalla,’ gum-lac, ‘dungries,’ red ‘dragons elaus,’ ‘dragon malies,’ ‘petafrangies,’ black ‘dragam selaws,’ ‘canigoalonies,’ ‘serasses corges,’ ‘wooven tapes,’ ‘tappe finnes,’ ‘anacke dragams,’ ‘gold thrid wrought in this cloth,’ diamonds (173, containing 329 ‘mangalines<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-07-fn-080" href="#sec-07-fn-080" data-fn-target="sec-07-fn-080" role="doc-noteref">3</a></sup>’), bezar stones, butter, &amp;c. Total 21,059 [pagodas] : 2 : 8⅛. (1 <em>p.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">William Biddulph and John Young [at Agra] to the Same [about September</span> 8], 1621<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-07-fn-081" href="#sec-07-fn-081" data-fn-target="sec-07-fn-081" role="doc-noteref">4</a></sup> (<em>Ibid., f.</em> 70).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">As regards the great jewel, they will obey Surat’s instructions to keep it till a higher price be offered or further orders come from the Company. Weight of the great ruby. The pictures they will sell at the best rates possible. All their quicksilver disposed of. The tusks are cracked and split, but they hope to find customers <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-321" data-page="321" data-folio="267" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories01fost/page/n320_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 267">[p. 267]</button> for them shortly. They regret to hear of the insolence of the Dutch, which they hope will in time be curbed. Letters from ‘Semana’ advise that [Robert Young] has 50 fardles of calico ready, and hopes to make them up to 70; also that John Willoughby has returned from Lahore with Rs. 4,000, after spending Rs. 1,000 in presents to recover that amount. For this expense they are not responsible, as they advised him ‘rather then to give anything of value, to leave what the rest was; yf not received in season to come with Mr. [Robert] Young hether, and wee would procure sufficient order for its recoverie’. ‘Our freinds in Brampoore advise us that Mounserechan<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-07-fn-082" href="#sec-07-fn-082" data-fn-target="sec-07-fn-082" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> is revolted from the Decanyes and is come to Brampoore to the Prince, where hee is receaved with great honnour.’ The business of Soares is likely to be revived, as he is expected at Burhānpur shortly. The factors wish for copies of the documents connected with the case. There is no copy of the Prince’s farmān at Agra; ‘therefore hope you can furnish them from thence; yf not, for a smale matter Afzullchans cheefe banian, whoe keepes register of all firmaens given, will give them coppies of both firmaens sent from hence about that businesse.’ They wish this troublesome matter well disposed of. ‘In a former wee gave you advertisement of divers Sinde marchaunts that weare come hither to complayne to the King that [wee had?] perforce taken their goods at sea [<em>see</em> p. 186]; and since they are growne [            ] in prosecuteing that businesse, in soe much that Asulphchan sent for us and wished to give them content, or elce they would complaine to the King, which would be much to our nations dishonner, and for our sakes hee hindered them from their purposes. Wee have absolutely denied to have any such passage, but that wee knew yf they had taken any goods it was the Portingalls, and these people onely subornd by them to complayne against us; but if they could proove that our people had taken any of this Kings subjects, after answer from Surratt wee should give them an answer, and wee doubted not but to content, either in monyes or reasons sufficient to the contrary. Whereupon they shewed the Nabob an inventory of all their goods that weare shipped in that shipp or boate, being taken out of the Customers books there and witnesed by the Customer and all his cheef officers. To which <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-322" data-page="322" data-folio="268" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories01fost/page/n321_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 268">[p. 268]</button> wee gave such answers as weare then necessary, and that wee could doe nothing therein untill received reply.' They request speedy instructions, as the complainants are pressing for justice. Thomas Hawkridge understood (and so did they) from previous letters that he had been promoted to a merchant's place, which he has well deserved for his pains taken so many years in these parts in the Honourable Company's service; as, however, it now appears that this was not the intention, they have tried to persuade him to continue an attendant, but he is very unwilling to do so. 'The 6th present the King's peshchanna <sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-07-fn-083" href="#sec-07-fn-083" data-fn-target="sec-07-fn-083" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> went out; and to-morrow the King himselfe departs this cittye <sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-07-fn-084" href="#sec-07-fn-084" data-fn-target="sec-07-fn-084" role="doc-noteref">2</a></sup>, and in his progresse is intended, as report saith, for Adgmeere and soe for Mandoe; but as yet noe certaintie.' Hughes has not yet arrived from Patna. [Robert] Young writes that if not hindered by the rains he and his goods will reach Agra the 10th proximo. 'By report this hundred yeares there hath not bin such extremitie of raynes, insoe much that most parte of the new indicoe drowned, and the old much improoved.' <em>P.S.</em>—Hawkridge started two days ago towards Patna to meet and hasten the factors and their goods. (<em>Copy. Much damaged.</em> 4 <em>pp.</em>)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">Robert Hughes and John Parker at Patna to the Factors at Agra, September</span> 13, 1621 (<em>Factory Records, Patna</em>, vol. i. p. 35).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Their letter of August 19 came last night. The orders from Surat <sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-07-fn-085" href="#sec-07-fn-085" data-fn-target="sec-07-fn-085" role="doc-noteref">3</a></sup> cannot now be carried out, as they have practically cleared all their business here and are just about to start. Hughes is to proceed at once, and Parker to follow with the carts in about four days time. They have been obliged to pay 2¼ rupees per maund for carriage to Agra 'carravan burbust' [<em>see</em> p. 248], and cannot hope to arrive before October 31. 'The raynes hath bin soe extraordynary this yeare that extraordinary charges cannott any way <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-323" data-page="323" data-folio="269" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories01fost/page/n322_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 269">[p. 269]</button> further our goods arryvall.’ They hope Thomas Hawkridge will meet Parker. (<em>Copy.</em> ½ <em>p.</em>)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">Edward Heynes and Robert Hutchinson at BurhĀnpur to the President and Council at Surat, September</span> 16, 1621 (<em>Brit. Mus. Egerton MS.</em> 2123, <em>f.</em> 49).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The renewed attempt of Soares to claim damages from the Company. He is not likely to succeed. Dispute with the natives over some indigo. ‘The Cassie [<em>see</em> p. 39] hath sent for testemonye from the registers of the chowtry [<em>see</em> p. 198] in that place<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-07-fn-086" href="#sec-07-fn-086" data-fn-target="sec-07-fn-086" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup>, whoe dayly take custome and record all bargaines past.’ They await Biddulph’s promised testimony ‘against the shraff in the business of Rampaxad [Ramprasād]’. There is little to advise regarding the sale of goods. They would be glad to know at what price the coral is selling at Surat, that they may tell what to ask for theirs. Not much demand for ivory. They trust to hear soon of the arrival of the fleet. They have arranged to remit money to Surat, though the exchange is dear; two bills enclosed. Red ‘birrams’ are too expensive to be bought. With regard to the charge of the Surat factors against Heynes, he ‘denyeth that hee hath any way taxt the Councell or strained his pen in conversation with them immodestly beyond due bounds or reason’. Copies of accounts sent. <em>P.S.</em>—Hutchinson requests to be credited with the value of five horses, ‘with their saddles, cost ma[hmūdīs] 221 : 18 pice, which, after the rate of 32 pice to the ma[hmūdī] and 80 pice to the rup[ee], as I keepe my accompts, amounteth to rup[ees] 88 : 50 pice.’ (<em>A much mutilated copy.</em> 7 <em>pp.</em>)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">John Parker at Patna to the Same, September</span> 17, 1621 (<em>Factory Records, Patna</em>, vol. i. p. 36).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Explains why the orders conveyed in their letter of July 14 for the continuance of the factory could not be carried out. All their arrangements had been made for departure, and his stay would have been a useless expense. Further, ‘though the way betweene this and Agra is nott very daingerous for robbers, yett nott free of taxes, as you may perceave by the transporte of our last goods from hence, which cost 14 rup[ees] per carte, and since other <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-324" data-page="324" data-folio="270" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories01fost/page/n323_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 270">[p. 270]</button> merchants have paid 200 [<em>sic</em>] rup[ees] per carte, soe that it is nott unrequizite that some Englishman accompany the goods, by whose presence the greatest parte or all may peradventure be saved, which I shall endeavour. Mr. Hughes departed hence the 13th current and went by the way of Lackhoare, to hasten away the cloth bought there to Mobulepoore<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-07-fn-087" href="#sec-07-fn-087" data-fn-target="sec-07-fn-087" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup>, which is theire place of ladinge ; and appointed me to make what hast I could and send away the goods here to meet them, which haveinge effected, to goe for Lackhoare to imbale four or five fardells yett unpacked and clere some small matters there, and thence to proceed in company of the goods with what speed possible for Agra.’ They recently bought fifty maunds of gum-lac, a few Malda wares for patterns for Persia, some ‘amberties’, and various stuffs as samples. (<em>Copy. 1½ pp.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">John Parker at Patna to the Factors at Agra</span>, <span class="sc">September</span> 17, 1621 (<em>Ibid.</em>, vol. i. p. 37).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Will start as soon as the rain permits, which ‘dayly powreth downe in such quantity that I cannott gett an hower of faire wether whereby to send forth the goods, which nowe is all ready for the carte’. (<em>Copy. ½ p.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">Edward Heynes and Robert Hutchinson at Burhānpur to the President and Council at Surat</span>, <span class="sc">September</span> [18 ?], 1621 (<em>Brit. Mus. Egerton MS. 2123, f.</em> 52).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Have received the testimony from Biddulph in the matter of ‘Herpaxad’s’ debt ; but the latter has no means, and so they leave the money to be recovered at Surat ‘by bribeing of your Governor’. The complaint from the Surat factors has been presented to ‘C[o]ja Abdala Hassan ’, who promised justice and ‘wished us rest content, for hee would mayntaine noe servant to wrong us or our nation’. Negotiations for sale of coral, &amp;c. They would be glad to be furnished with a broker who will give his whole attention to their business. The present one ‘hath deluded us’ in the buying of gum-lac, ‘hee haveing made a mempoke thereof under culler by others, in soe much that either wee must pay dearer then wee formerly advised you, or elce rely on his performances.’ They request to know what articles are to be inserted in the Prince’s farmān, promised at Is-hāq Beg’s arrival. (<em>A damaged copy. 2¼ pp.</em>)</p></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-325"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="325" data-folio="271" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories01fost/page/n324_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 271">[p. 271]</button></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">John Weddell</span><sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-07-fn-088" href="#sec-07-fn-088" data-fn-target="sec-07-fn-088" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup>, <span class="sc">Nicholas Bangham, James Beversham, Joseph Salbank, and William Hoare, aboard the</span> <em>Jonas</em> <span class="sc">off Damān, to the President and Council at Surat, September</span> 18, 1621 (<em>Ibid.</em>, <em>f.</em> 48).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">They set sail from England the [     ] of April, 1621; did not touch at the Cape, but refreshed at ‘Augustine<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-07-fn-089" href="#sec-07-fn-089" data-fn-target="sec-07-fn-089" role="doc-noteref">2</a></sup> and Joanna<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-07-fn-090" href="#sec-07-fn-090" data-fn-target="sec-07-fn-090" role="doc-noteref">3</a></sup>’; and heard by the <em>Rose</em> of Pring’s departure for England in the <em>Royal James</em> in June last. They have only lost fourteen men in the whole fleet. They would be glad of some fresh victuals. (<em>A much mutilated copy. 1 p.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">Richard Swanley’s</span><sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-07-fn-091" href="#sec-07-fn-091" data-fn-target="sec-07-fn-091" role="doc-noteref">4</a></sup> <span class="sc">Account of his Voyage to Surat</span> (<em>Marine Records</em>, vol. xxxiv).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">1621, March 19. Sailed from Tilbury. May 30. Saw the island of Trinidada.<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-07-fn-092" href="#sec-07-fn-092" data-fn-target="sec-07-fn-092" role="doc-noteref">5</a></sup> June 30. Sighted ‘the land of the Table Bay’, but continued their voyage, as they had already lost much time. July 4. Overtaken by the <em>Richard</em> and the <em>Rose</em>, both outward bound.<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-07-fn-093" href="#sec-07-fn-093" data-fn-target="sec-07-fn-093" role="doc-noteref">6</a></sup> July 23. Anchored in St. Augustine’s Bay [Madagascar]. ‘Here wee had great store of fresh victles, as beefes, sheepe, goats, and hennes, as much as wee would desire and very good cheape, for the people would take noe money but silver chaines and counterfett bludstones. There wee had for a sillver chaine of the matter of a foott long, which cost in England some five or six shillings, a good beefe which wer worth in England some four or five pounds; and a goate or a sheepe for four or five bludstones. The people there are very bould, for they would come abord and eate and drinke <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-326" data-page="326" data-folio="272" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories01fost/page/n325_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 272">[p. 272]</button> with us, and they would take any thing that wee would give them ; but wee could never see that they wer treacherus or to steale any thinge from us (but very laiing).' August 1. The <em>Richard</em> was dispatched to sail along the coast to discover whether there was any good harbour to the northwards. August 2. The fleet sailed. August 12. Anchored at Joanna, one of the Comoro islands. August 16. The <em>Richard</em> rejoined. August 18. The fleet sailed. September 4. 'This day there was a feast abord the <em>Dolphine</em>. Being very merry in the evening, when the captain came from thence, there was three peeces shott off. So it pleased God that with one of the peeces Hugh Woodcock, being in the skiffe under the peece, was shott over, that noe man knewe what was become of him, but a peece of his capp was founde in the skiffe sterne.' September 12. 'This day in the afternoone wee saw great store of snackes, which a man shall commonly see when hee is within thirty or forty leages of the land.' September 15. 'From the last day of August to this day in the morning wee bore a slack saile to linger out the tyme for coming upon the coast of Surratt, for feare of fowle weather, which commonly contynueth untill the 16 or 20 of September.' September 21. Anchored outside Swally sands. September 23. The President and merchants came on board. September 24. The <em>Richard</em> arrived with a Portuguese prize of 200 tons, laden mostly with ivory.<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-07-fn-094" href="#sec-07-fn-094" data-fn-target="sec-07-fn-094" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> September 26. The <em>Jonas</em> went into the road. September 27. The <em>Whale</em>, <em>Dolphin</em>, and prize came over the bar. September 29.<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-07-fn-095" href="#sec-07-fn-095" data-fn-target="sec-07-fn-095" role="doc-noteref">2</a></sup> 'The <em>Andrew</em>, Mr. Pinder being master, brought in a prize, which was a juncke of the Mellickambers, which he had taken coming from the Red Sea, <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-327" data-page="327" data-folio="273" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories01fost/page/n326_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 273">[p. 273]</button> upon consideration of wrongs which hee had donne unto our marchants in Mogulls cuntry by robing their caffely [<em>see</em> p. 31 <em>n.</em>] in coming downe from Agra.' October 20. Captain Blyth's fleet came into the road with two prizes. (30 <em>pp.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">William Martin, George Pike, and John Glanvill at Broach to the President and Council at Surat, September</span> 23, 1621 (<em>Brit. Mus. Egerton MS.</em> 2123, <em>f.</em> 53).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Acknowledge the receipt of certain bills of exchange. The Governor's 'sheckdare' [<em>see</em> p. 205] is pressing them for an answer about the indigo, and they would therefore be glad of instructions in the matter. (<em>Copy.</em> ¾ <em>p.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3">[<span class="sc">Edward Heynes</span>] <span class="sc">and Robert</span> [<span class="sc">Hutchinson</span>] <span class="sc">at Burhānpur to the Same, September</span> 26, 1621 (<em>Ibid.</em>, <em>f.</em> 57).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Their petition for justice against Soares was handed to the Prince in the 'Gushall Chonna<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-07-fn-096" href="#sec-07-fn-096" data-fn-target="sec-07-fn-096" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup>', as also a purse for a present. He seemed much pleased with that 'topha' [<em>see</em> p. 126 <em>n.</em>], and, after questioning Soares, referred the dispute to Afzal Khān. He in turn sent them to 'the Cassie [<em>see</em> p. 39 <em>n.</em>], who, upon examination of both sides, found Swaries testamonyes falce and his demands unjust; wee unreasonable to require satisfaction from him of such monyes as wee had disbursed and damages sustayned; and concluded that Swaries ought not to trouble us or our masters goods, nor wee him, if wee dispended a lack of rupies in defence thereof. Wee desired his scrite [<em>see</em> p. 4 <em>n.</em>] in testimony of this sentence, that in after tymes wee might prevent further trouble, which hee denieth to doe or graunt us untill hee hath made earrs<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-07-fn-097" href="#sec-07-fn-097" data-fn-target="sec-07-fn-097" role="doc-noteref">2</a></sup> for approbation thereof, the Prince being first possessed with the cause and referd to him by his order. Wee must and will attend to have this sentence under record, although it might be well passed over with what hath been donn therein, for that Swaries is miserable poore, despised of all men, disrespected by the Prince; yet hee continueues his religion, and constant therein, and in which hee onely deserves comendations.' The 'books of chowtrie in Hassenpoore' [<em>see</em> p. 269] confirmed the</p></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-328"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="328" data-folio="274" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories01fost/page/n327_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 274">[p. 274]</button></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">‘Banians books’; so the ‘Cassie’ does not know what to do. Advice as to the settlement of the dispute. By the ‘casaina<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-07-fn-098" href="#sec-07-fn-098" data-fn-target="sec-07-fn-098" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup>’ they received the amber and scales and have credited Surat accordingly. ‘Upon the arivall of Isack Beag (whome wee have vizited according to your orders), wee solicited anew Afzullchon for the Princes firmaen, promised at first in presence of many hombras<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-07-fn-099" href="#sec-07-fn-099" data-fn-target="sec-07-fn-099" role="doc-noteref">2</a></sup>, both of King and Prince. With much earnestnes he tould us that [he ?] had receaved letters from the Mellik Amber that our shipps had taken a junck of his comeing from the Red Sea : that Cojah Abdala Hassan<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-07-fn-100" href="#sec-07-fn-100" data-fn-target="sec-07-fn-100" role="doc-noteref">3</a></sup> had received advize from Surrat that [               ] taken out of the Pr[               ] pretences they knew not [            ] unreasonnable and trouble [               ] at first to have sought rest[itution      ] by way of peticion to the Mellick and [               ] before wee had taken such violent course [               ] weare soe troublesome and unprofitable that the P[rince had] resolved to give us leave to departe his kingdom [? and if not, to] commaund us forth by force, if [we] would not live as [               ] and discreet marchaunts. To which wee replyed [with the li]ke earnestnes that notwithstanding the tyme of w[inter] had made the wayes unsafe and unpassable for any of our nation to travell to the court of the Mellick with securitie, yet wee had indevored to make our recourse to his ambassador in sute (?) of satisfaction according to equitie, but could not be admitted to his presence, hee denieing conversation with us, and onely sent a scornefull and proud messuadges [<em>sic</em>] in liue of justice or restitucion ; affirmeing that the Mellicks and [<em>sic</em>] unhonnorable action by breatch of his word and cowle<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-07-fn-101" href="#sec-07-fn-101" data-fn-target="sec-07-fn-101" role="doc-noteref">4</a></sup> induced us to soe a setled resolution as to requite him with force and violence in soe that [not ?] a shipp or boate should passe to and from any of his ports untill wee weare fully satisfied. Wee further said that noe man knew better then himselfe the insufferable injuries wee had suffered this many yeares together, as well from the Princes servants and officers in Surratt, as from others the King and Princes subjects elce where : that wee weare not onely abused in our persons, dispoyled of our goods, but debarred of our trade, which hath been <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-329" data-page="329" data-folio="275" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories01fost/page/n328_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 275">[p. 275]</button> and now is graunted us by their sundry firmaens extant: that for these wee have often sought by petition for justice and reformacion at the hands of the King, Prince, and himselfe, and as yet could receave none, but rather more wrong heaped upon our sufferings, which we could not longer indure, but must be playne and give them to understand that wee intend to right our wrongs by our owne force, seeing wee cannot finde justice by the Prince, to whome wee are dayly sutors: that as wee came into this country by virtue of the Kings firmaen authorizing us, soe should' [<em>part torn away</em>].
