Although Kerridge's name only is given, it is the Surat factors' formal letter to the Company, narrating all that had happened since the previous spring.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-052" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-052" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">The origin of the term 'dimity' is still doubtful: see the remarks in the <em>Oxford English Dictionary</em>.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-053" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-053" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Dunnage; i. e. for placing under or between the bales in stowing them in the ship, to prevent chafing or damage by water.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-054" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-054" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">The ‘Treaty of Defence’ between the Dutch and the English.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-055" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-055" aria-label="Return to text">3.</a> <span class="fn-body">One of the earliest references to what afterwards became an important branch of the Company’s commerce. We know from the Court Minutes that in England, while the demand for saltpetre was increasing, the supply from the usual sources was diminishing; hence, probably, their inquiry as to the possibility of obtaining a supply from India.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-056" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-056" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Probably Abraham de Duyts, a Dutch jeweller in Shāh Jahān’s service (<em>see The Embassy of Sir T. Roe</em>, p. 442).</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-057" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-057" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">In salutes, which were fired on the arrival and departure of visitors, drinking of healths, &amp;c.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-058" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-058" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">This term appears in an earlier letter, where the Patna factors, writing to Agra, say: ‘You maye thanke Shame [Shyām] for his cassmanna, though his letter of credditt was of no validitye.’ It is probably the Hind. <em>khās nāma</em>, ‘a letter of introduction,’ referring to some testimony of their trustworthiness which the factors carried with them to Patna to facilitate their dealings with the shroffs at the latter place. On p. 248, it seems to refer to the general farmān given to the English by Jahāngīr.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-059" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-059" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">A copy made in 1788. It is not clear whether the date of this document is Old Style or New.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-060" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-060" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Cf. a letter from the Dutch chief at Masulipatam, Andries Souri, dated May 22 (N.S.), 1621, in the <em>Hague Transcripts</em> (1st Series, vol. iv. No. 139). He mentions the arrival of the <em>Bear</em> and <em>Medemblick</em>; the coming of a French factor from Achin, who applied to the Governor for permission to establish a factory at Masulipatam, but was told that the request could not be considered until Beaulieu brought his ships thither; the dispersal by the Portuguese of a fleet of native boats bringing carpets from Bengal to sell at Masulipatam; the establishment of the Danes at Tranquebar; the receipt of an invitation from 'Mirjhasaj', Mogul Governor of 'Mamenipatan', to the Dutch to settle in his port; it lies in 20½ or 21 degrees, and fifteen to twenty miles from Pipplī, and was formerly a place of importance, though now decayed owing to the wars, &amp;c. &amp;c.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-061" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-061" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">See p. 208. Purchas calls Methwold’s companion ‘Sir Andreas Socory, Governor of the Fort, Guide in Pallecat’: for which we should read ‘Signor Andreas Soury, Governour of the Fort Geldria in Pallecat’.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-062" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-062" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">These ships belonged to Beaulieu’s fleet, which sailed from Honfleur in October, <span class="folio-turn">[p. 256]</span> 1619: <em>see Memoires du Voyage du General Beaulieu</em>, in Thevenot's <em>Relations de divers Voyages Curieux</em> (1696).</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-063" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-063" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body"><em>See Hague Transcripts</em>, 1st Series, vol. iv. No. 138.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-064" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-064" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Hughes notes that he wrote at the same time and to the same effect to Surat.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-065" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-065" aria-label="Return to text">3.</a> <span class="fn-body">Hind. <em>takā</em>, a somewhat varying fraction of a rupee: two pice in Hindūstān, three in Gujarāt, and four in the Deccan, while commonly in Bengal the word is used for the rupee itself.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-066" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-066" aria-label="Return to text">4.</a> <span class="fn-body">Prince Parwīz (<em>see</em> p. 236).</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-067" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-067" aria-label="Return to text">5.</a> <span class="fn-body">By paying a high price.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-068" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-068" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">A jeweller’s weight, equivalent to four <em>māshas</em>.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-069" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-069" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">The original letter (much damaged) forms <em>O. C.</em> 981. It bears the impress of a seal, with a Persian inscription giving Hughes’s name with the addition of <em>saudāgar-i-Angrīz</em> (English merchant).</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-070" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-070" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Hind. <em>parwāna</em>, ‘a written order.’</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-071" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-071" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">The Nawāb Āsaf Khān.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-072" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-072" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Hind. <em>daftar</em>, a ‘register’ or ‘account’.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-073" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-073" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Hind. <em>mīr-sāmān</em>, ‘head-steward.’</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-074" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-074" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Eight viss made a maund, and twenty maunds one candy.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-075" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-075" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Hind. <em>dalālī</em>, ‘brokerage,’ ‘commission.’</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-076" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-076" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Pieter Gillesz. van Ravesteyn had formerly been the Dutch chief at Surat (<em>see</em> p. 15) and was now returning thither from Jakatra, via Masulipatam and the Red Sea.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-077" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-077" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Sukadana, in Borneo.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-078" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-078" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">The <em>Unicorn</em>, proceeding in company with the <em>Royal James</em> from Bantam to Japan, was towards the end of June, 1620, driven by a storm on the coast of China not far from Macao. The crew got safely on shore, and were thus the first Englishmen to land on the coast of the Celestial Empire. They were well treated by the natives, who sold them two vessels in which to make their way to Jakatra. A second storm, however, separated the pair, and while one succeeded in reaching its destination, the other was captured by the Portuguese and taken into Macao. The crew were imprisoned, but the barber-surgeon, here referred to, in return for medical service to the daughter of the Governor, was set at liberty and made his way to Masulipatam in May, 1621. See <em>Purchas his Pilgrimes</em>, vol. i. p. 642, vol. ii. p. 1696; <em>Hague Transcripts</em> at I. O., 1st Series, vol. iv. No. 139; <em>Cocks’s Diary</em>, vol. ii.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-079" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-079" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Malayālam <em>channātam</em>, ‘a double-platform canoe made by placing a floor of boards across two boats, with a bamboo railing’; hence also ‘a raft’ (Port. <em>jangada</em>). Cf. <em>Purchas</em>, vol. i. p. 315 (‘there came aboard two <em>gingathas</em> or boats’) and p. 631 (‘their boat being split in pieces, made a <em>gingada</em> of timber’).</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-080" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-080" aria-label="Return to text">3.</a> <span class="fn-body">Telegu <em>manjali</em>, the seed of <em>Adenanthera pavonina</em>, used as a weight by jewellers. It was about equivalent to a carat.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-081" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-081" aria-label="Return to text">4.</a> <span class="fn-body">The copy is not dated, but it is marked as received October 2. Letters from Agra to Surat usually took from twenty-five to thirty days, and from internal evidence the date appears to be as given above.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-082" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-082" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Mansūr Khān, described later as a ‘Portugall renegado’.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-083" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-083" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Pers. <em>pīsh-khāna</em>, 'advance-service,' i. e. tents sent on for erection at the next stopping-place.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-084" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-084" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">He did not really leave till Ābān 12 = October 22 (see Elliot and Dowson's <em>History</em>, vol. vi. p. 381). Such delays were common enough, as for one thing it was always necessary to wait for a lucky day.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-085" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-085" aria-label="Return to text">3.</a> <span class="fn-body">For the factory to be continued, Parker remaining in charge until the arrival of Young from Agra.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-086" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-086" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">‘Hassenpoore, adjoyneing to Brampoore.’ The name has not been traced in the <em>Indian Atlas</em> sheet of the district.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-087" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-087" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Perhaps Mohub Alīpur, near Mussowrah. Evidently the factors took the lower road from Patna to Benares (through Sāhserām).</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-088" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-088" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Went out in Bonner’s fleet of 1618 as master of the <em>Lion</em>, returning in her to England in September, 1620. He was thereupon promoted to the command of the fleet for the following year. A brief account of his distinguished after-career will be found in the <em>Dict. of Nat. Biography.</em></span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-089" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-089" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">A bay on the south-west coast of Madagascar.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-090" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-090" aria-label="Return to text">3.</a> <span class="fn-body">One of the Comoro Islands.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-091" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-091" aria-label="Return to text">4.</a> <span class="fn-body">He was master or master’s mate on the <em>Jonas</em>, Captain Weddell’s flagship. The other vessels of the fleet were the <em>Whale</em>, the <em>Dolphin</em>, and the <em>Lion.</em></span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-092" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-092" aria-label="Return to text">5.</a> <span class="fn-body">The small volcanic island of that name in the South Atlantic, 700 miles from Brazil. For Capt. Best’s account of a visit to it in 1612 see <em>Letters Received</em>, vol. vi. p. 291.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-093" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-093" aria-label="Return to text">6.</a> <span class="fn-body">Of 20 and 100 tons respectively. They had sailed from England in January, 1621, in company with the <em>Anne</em>, the <em>Lesser James</em>, and the <em>Fortune</em>. These three were bound for Bantam, while the <em>Richard</em> and the <em>Rose</em> were intended for ‘discovery abought the Cape of Good Hoope’ (Wood’s journal, <em>Marine Records</em>, vol. xxxiii). Weddell had been instructed to take them on to Surat should he meet with them.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-094" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-094" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">This capture is described in an undated letter printed at p. 729 of Purchas's first volume. 'It is a wonder to tell it, the <em>Richard</em> being a pinnace of about twentie tunnes, manned with fourteene men and boyes, and having but two small falconets in her, and the Portugall of two hundred and fiftie tunnes, with two hundred and fiftie in her, whereof seventie were Portugalls, the rest mesticos and slaves. The <em>Richard</em>, comming up with them, plyed her demi-dogs so well that in the shooting off twentie shot, it pleased God they killed their captayne; which the rest perceiving stroke all their sailes amayne, and like tall men came aboard the <em>Richard</em>, sixe at a time, where the master caused them to be bound; but fearing to have too many aboard, though bound, he sent a terrible command to the rest aboard, that upon their lives they should depart their ship into their great boat; which they very valiantly performed, and left their ship, to the utter disgrace of themselves and all their nation.' A rather longer account of the exploit is given in Purchas's second volume, p. 1791.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-095" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-095" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">This date appears to be wrong (cf. p. 286).</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-096" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-096" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Hind. <em>ghusl-khāna</em>, strictly 'a bath-room', but often applied, as here, to the room in which the King or Prince gave private audience (cf. Constable's <em>Bernier</em>, p. 265).</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-097" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-097" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Petition (Hind. <em>arz</em>).</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-098" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-098" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Treasure-caravan (see note on p. 91).</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-099" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-099" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Grandees (Arabic <em>umarā</em>, really the pl. of <em>amīr</em>).</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-100" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-100" aria-label="Return to text">3.</a> <span class="fn-body">Khwāja Abdala Hasan.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-101" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-101" aria-label="Return to text">4.</a> <span class="fn-body">Safe conduct (Ar. <em>qaul</em>).</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-102" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-102" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Jan van Gorcom, afterwards member of council at Batavia (1626).</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-103" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-103" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">There is a Dutch copy among the <em>Hague Transcripts</em> (Series III, vol. i. No. M 1).</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-104" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-104" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Van Ravesteyn (see p. 264). The <em>Sampson</em> (the ship in which Jourdain had been killed) had been retained by the Dutch in compensation for their <em>Black Lion</em>, burnt while in the possession of the English.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-105" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-105" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Possibly <em>tarī</em> (cf. p. 106).</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-106" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-106" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Mosque (the Arabic <em>masjid</em> being in India softened into <em>masīd</em>).</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-107" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-107" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Kerridge was on board Swan’s vessel. For another account of this voyage see Pinder’s narrative (<em>supra</em>, p. 220 <em>n.</em>).</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-108" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-108" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Ras Madraka, the south-west point of the Gulf of Masīra.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-109" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-109" aria-label="Return to text">3.</a> <span class="fn-body">Masīra Island, off the south-east coast of Omān, between Ras Madraka and Ras-al-hadd.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-110" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-110" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Sūr, about fifteen miles west of Ras-al-hadd.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-111" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-111" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">See p. 288. 'Teve' is Taiwī, eight miles south of Ras-ash-Shajar.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-112" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-112" aria-label="Return to text">3.</a> <span class="fn-body">Dom Affonso de Noronha was appointed Viceroy and sailed in April, 1621, but was forced to put back. The new King then named Dom Francisco da Gama for the post, who started in March, 1622, and reached Goa the following September.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-113" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-113" aria-label="Return to text">4.</a> <span class="fn-body">Swan's next entry is for November 21, when, in company with the <em>Roebuck</em> and the Persia fleet, the <em>Hart</em> left Swally for England with Kerridge on board.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-114" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-114" aria-label="Return to text">5.</a> <span class="fn-body">See p. 203. He seems to have been now in the <em>Hart</em>.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-115" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-115" aria-label="Return to text">6.</a> <span class="fn-body">'On the fourteenth came order from Muscat to the Arabs to withstand our watering. With the Portugalls aide they did their best, some seven or eight hundred of them with <span class="folio-turn">[p. 286]</span> small shot and bow and arrowes; but the Lord fought our battell, so that but one of our men were lost; yet landed we daily with brasse base and small shot untill all our ships were watered’ (Pinder, <em>ut supra</em>).</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-116" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-116" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Khor Jarāma, three and a half miles west of Ras-al-hadd. It is described in the <em>Persian Gulf Pilot</em> as ‘a fine basin with a tortuous entrance a mile long and only 150 yards broad, between cliffs 60 feet high’. It seems a pity that the old name is not preserved in our charts to commemorate the visit of Blyth’s squadron.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-117" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-117" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">See <em>Hobson-Jobson</em>, 2nd edn., p. 343.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-118" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-118" aria-label="Return to text">3.</a> <span class="fn-body">‘The thirtieth of September . . . I was sent to goe for Surat in the <em>Andrew</em>, by order from Captaine Blith, and to advertise Master Thomas Rastell of our passage, he being chiefe in the factorie; and in my passage for Surrat I surprised a ship of that princes which had formerly wronged our masters, and carried her to Surrat, surrendering her to the chief factor’ (Pinder, <em>ut supra</em>).</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-119" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-119" aria-label="Return to text">4.</a> <span class="fn-body">The remainder of Jemison’s journal is occupied with the return voyage to England in the <em>Hart</em>.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-120" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-120" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">The first part of the letter is a copy of Jeffries’ letter of March 14, 1621 (see p. 241).</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-121" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-121" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Bālāghāt, the country 'above the passes' (over the Western Ghauts)—the Deccan.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-122" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-122" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">'Haughty' is here used in its literal sense of 'high'.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-123" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-123" aria-label="Return to text">3.</a> <span class="fn-body">Demurrage.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-124" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-124" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Fleming (Dutch).</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-125" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-125" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">The title of the Hindu sovereign of Calicut. For its origin see <em>Hobson-Jobson</em>, 2nd edn., p. 977.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-126" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-126" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">i. e. the Pole. He was probably the 'Polack' mentioned by Roe (<em>Embassy</em>, p. 355). His companion was no doubt the 'Sebastiano Fiorino' alluded to above, p. 223.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-127" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-127" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Mint (Hind. <em>taksāl</em>).</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-128" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-128" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Gujarātī <em>gajiyā</em>, cotton or silk cloth used for lining. The name is said to be derived from the Persian <em>gaj</em> or <em>gaz</em>, a measure of varying lengths.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-129" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-129" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">At the Bar of Surat, to watch for the returning Surat junk.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-130" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-130" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">The position of this letter in the book, as well as its contents, suggests that the date should really be the 15th.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-131" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-131" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Maize (Arabic <em>khandarūs</em>).</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-132" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-132" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Went out to India in 1617 as purser’s mate in the <em>Bull</em>, and returned in her at the end of the following year. In 1620 he was appointed purser of the <em>London</em>, and later on was made a factor. In Feb., 1624, his brother Ellis petitioned the Company to grant him an increase of wages, and he was then ‘commended to be a very honnest, sufficient yong man’. Soon after, however, came the news that Nicholas had been lost in the wreck of the <em>Whale</em> off Surat in March, 1623. On Oct. 5, 1624, administration was granted to Ellis Crispe for the estate of his brother Nicholas ‘late of Allhallows, Lombard Street’; and later (March, 1628), Ellis having died, fresh letters of administration were issued to an uncle Nicholas. The celebrated Sir Nicholas Crispe was probably a cousin.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-133" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-133" aria-label="Return to text">3.</a> <span class="fn-body">The ‘Dewghur’ of the Indian Atlas; a village about two miles from Chaul.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-134" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-134" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Sīdī Ambar, Sīdī Yāqūt, Sīdī Raihān.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-135" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-135" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Sīdī Surūr.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-136" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-136" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Not extant; but it is evidently the last of the letters abstracted in the following ‘Notes regarding the English factory at Pulicat’ (<em>O. C.</em> 885):— ‘9 Aprill, 1621. The <em>Globe</em> sett saile from Jaquatra towards Pellicatt, and in hir Thomas Mills and John Milward went in hir to reside at Pellicatt. (<em>From President Fursland's journal.</em>) ‘9 Juni. Thomas Mill[s] and John Milward arived at Pellicatt, and nothing could then be executed there on our partes till the Governour for the Dutch in that place should there arive from Musilpatam. (<em>Letter from Mills, July 10, 1621.</em>) ‘The Dutch pretende debts standing out in Pellicatt for 2 or 3000 pagodes. (<em>Ditto, July 16, 1621.</em>) ‘Ours in Pellicate write that they had paid our moyety of the charges in Pellicate for theis three monethes past, vizt. 940 <sup>3</sup>/<sub>8</sub> [rials]: ‘For the souldiours pay, three monethes, 871½ r[ial]s. ‘For the Forte charges, 68⅞ r[ial]s, besides the expence of powder, shott, and other munition, to be brought to accompt afterwardes. That the Dutch do put in and out souldiors at their pleasure without our knowledge or consent. (<em>Ditto, September 22, 1621.</em>)’</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-137" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-137" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">On Aug. 12, 1621 [N.S.] the Dutch Governor-General wrote to his subordinate at Masulipatam (<em>Hague Transcripts</em>, Series III. vol. i. No. L 1) directing him to discontinue <span class="folio-turn">[p. 299]</span> the practice, at Pulicat and elsewhere, of buying cloth jointly with the English: ‘We are not bound to do so by the contract, and we do not consider it advisable to bind ourselves in the matter; so do your best, without making the English any wiser than they are. We again warn you not to trust them in the least, for we find it productive of no good. It is also desirable that they should live outside rather than inside the fort. Do not let them infringe on our jurisdiction, honour, and prerogative. Make them pay from month to month the half of all expenses of the fort and garrison of Pulicat, and do not agree to the payment of any portion here (unless it should be to your advantage). In this way we shall avoid the necessity of running after the English, and they on the other hand will have to come to us.’</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-138" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-138" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">The term <em>Grāsiā</em> was originally applied to ‘a military and predatory chief in Malwa, Rajputana, Guzerat, and Cutch, claiming a portion of the revenues of certain villages, either as a grant originally from the superior authority, in requital of military service, or as the price of forbearance from plunder’ (<em>Wilson's Glossary</em>). It was, however, extended to mean any kind of blackmailer or freebooter; see the quotations in <em>Hobson-Jobson</em>, 2nd edn., p. 395.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-139" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-139" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Not identified.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-140" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-140" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">‘September’ in the MS.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-141" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-141" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Viz. of ‘Cats’, according to Van den Broecke’s letter in <em>Hague Transcripts</em> (Series I. vol. iv. No. 140). This is probably the ‘Cuts-nagore’ of Downton and the ‘Cache’ of Bocarro, identified by Sir Henry Yule (<em>Hobson-Jobson</em>, p. 286) with Mandvī, in Kutch. Van den Broecke says that the persons on board were set ashore on the Arabian coast. He confirms the statement that this and other vessels taken had passes from the Dutch factory at Mokha, and forwards a copy of a strong remonstrance he had received from the Governor of Dābhol.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-142" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-142" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">It will be remembered that he came out with Roe as his secretary.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-143" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-143" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Yacht (the Dutch <em>Naerden</em>).</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-144" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-144" aria-label="Return to text">3.</a> <span class="fn-body">Afterwards the site of the English settlement called Fort St. David. It is nearly a hundred miles from St. Thomé.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-145" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-145" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">See Methwold's narrative in <em>Purchas his Pilgrimage</em>, p. 994.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-146" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-146" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Mokha.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-147" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-147" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">The point where the road from Baroda crossed the Mahī, near Vāsad. This was probably the limit of the jurisdiction of the Governor of Ahmadābād.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-148" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-148" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Not identified.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-149" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-149" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Sakhera or Sankheda, about twenty miles south-east of Baroda.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-150" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-150" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Perhaps the ‘Sambelott’ of p. 308.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-151" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-151" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Not identified.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-152" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-152" aria-label="Return to text">3.</a> <span class="fn-body">The late English chief at Ahmadābād.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-153" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-153" aria-label="Return to text">4.</a> <span class="fn-body">He writes it ‘Cambayett’ (cf. <em>Hobson-Jobson</em>, p. 150).</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-154" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-154" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Bale (Hind. <em>basta</em>).</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-155" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-155" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">As hostage for Jeffries and his companions (see p. 290).</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-156" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-156" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body"><em>Rūnās</em>, Indian madder.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-157" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-157" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Apparently the ‘pledge’ referred to on p. 310 (Arabic <em>qabīl</em>, ‘a surety’).</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-158" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-158" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">It must be remembered that Blyth and Weddell were in command of independent fleets, and the question of precedence arose if their ships were to act together.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-159" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-159" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Probably the Pole mentioned on p. 291.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-160" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-160" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Servant to Edward Connock, whom he accompanied to Persia. After his master’s death he appears to have been sent back to Surat (see p. 140).</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-161" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-161" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Not identified. Probably some village in which Malik Ambar had temporarily established his head quarters.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-162" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-162" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Junnar, in Poona district, seventy miles north-east of Bombay. It was there that Dr. Fryer visited the Nawāb Mukhlis Khān in 1675.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-163" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-163" aria-label="Return to text">3.</a> <span class="fn-body">Daulatābād, the famous fortress, anciently known as Deogiri, which has played a leading part in the history of the Deccan from the earliest times. It is situated ten miles to the north-west of Aurangābād.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-164" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-164" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Mr. Joshi suggests that this is a mistake for the Gujarātī <em>mehta</em>, an accountant or secretary.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-165" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-165" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Lakhs. Jeffries was no doubt making a liberal estimate of the money that the goods would have produced in England.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-166" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-166" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Ibrāhīm Adil Shāh II of Bijāpūr. His distrust of Malik Ambar is mentioned in a letter from the Governor of Goa to King Philip, dated Feb. 18, 1622, among the <em>Lisbon Transcripts</em>.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-167" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-167" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">The presentation of pawn was of course merely a ceremony of leave-taking, and did not imply any ‘love’ for the visitor.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-168" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-168" aria-label="Return to text">3.</a> <span class="fn-body"><em>Note in margin.</em> ‘This murther and robbery was done by the Dutch in the <em>Sampson</em> and another shipp and fathered upon the English.’</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-169" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-169" aria-label="Return to text">4.</a> <span class="fn-body">Aurangābād, which was at this time known as Kirkī, the name given to it by its founder, Malik Ambar.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-170" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-170" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Probably Jādū Rāy (see p. 332).</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-171" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-171" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Not identified.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-172" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-172" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">An early example of this incorrect plural.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-173" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-173" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Mohur. In Elkington's notebook, 1614–15 (<em>Factory Records, Miscell.</em>, vol. xxv) there is a note that the gold 'mahoores' then current were of four sorts, worth 25, 12½, 10, and 5 rupees respectively.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-174" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-174" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">See note on p. 90.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-175" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-175" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Probably an error for <em>dig-lac</em> (Mahr. <em>dig</em>, 'crude').</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-176" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-176" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">There is another abstract of the same letter under <em>O. C.</em> 1009, but it is not so full.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-177" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-177" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">To 'play <em>rex</em>' meant to act as a king, do anything you pleased. Nares in his <em>Glossary</em> quotes from Du Bartas : 'Then plaies he rex; tears, kils, and all consumes.'</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-178" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-178" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">See Van den Broecke's letter referred to on p. 300. The English and Dutch failed to come to a satisfactory agreement, and so the <em>Sampson</em> remained behind.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-179" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-179" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">‘For the Prince desireth it; on whose satisfaction in such services dependes his favors to us’ (<em>O. C.</em> 1009).</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-180" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-180" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">This is explained in <em>O. C.</em> 1009 to mean from his relinquishing his post as President to his return to Surat.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-181" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-181" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Otherwise the <em>Dragon’s Claw</em>.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-182" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-182" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">The rest of the letter is concerned with affairs at Achin, Batavia, &amp;c.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-183" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-183" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Apparently some kind of piece-goods.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-184" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-184" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body"><em>Rāyat</em> (ryot), a ‘subject’, particularly a farmer or a peasant.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-185" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-185" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Not extant.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-186" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-186" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Himmat Khān, Governor of Broach.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-187" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-187" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Presumably on his birthday (January 5, O. S.).</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-188" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-188" aria-label="Return to text">3.</a> <span class="fn-body">See note on p. 208.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-189" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-189" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">The celebrated military leader, Khān Jahān Lōdī.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-190" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-190" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Mauritius (see <em>Purchas his Pilgrimage</em>, p. 780). For the loss of the <em>Samaritan</em>, see a note on p. 67.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-191" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-191" aria-label="Return to text">3.</a> <span class="fn-body">Kishm, on the eastern point of the island of that name, opposite to Ormus.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-192" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-192" aria-label="Return to text">4.</a> <span class="fn-body">Apparently German, &amp;c., dollars are meant. Often the term was used as synonymous with 'piece of eight'.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-193" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-193" aria-label="Return to text">5.</a> <span class="fn-body">'Haberdin' (French <em>habordéan</em>) was the old name for salt cod. By 'Newland fish' Wheatley means the salted cod of the Newfoundland fisheries, sometimes called 'bacalow' (Port. <em>bacalhão</em>).</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-194" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-194" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">'This courte being made acquainted that wormwood beere is very excelent, wholsom, and soveraigne for preservacion of mens healthes at sea, and yett no charge (or very little) unto the Companie, they appoynted Mr. Mountney to take care that two hogsheads for each shippe be prepared and put into them for their uses' (<em>Court Minutes</em>, Nov. 20, 1618).</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-195" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><strong class="fn-backlink fn-unlinked">2 · [Unlinked note, p. 338]</strong> <span class="fn-body">Some kind of silk cloth; see <em>Hobson-Jobson</em>, s. v. 'soosie'.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-196" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-196" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Hind. <em>khazānchī</em>, 'treasurer,' 'cashier.'</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-197" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-197" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">i. e. ‘gunny-ed’ (covered with gunny).</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-198" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-198" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Specimens of the Company’s bale-mark are figured in Sir George Birdwood’s <em>Report on the Old Records of the India Office</em>, pp. 72, 73.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-199" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-199" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Only the portions relating to the Indian factories are here dealt with.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-200" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-200" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">To take prizes.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-201" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-201" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">See note on p. 298.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-202" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-202" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">General letter (see the preceding document).</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-203" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-203" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Apparently a play upon the words <em>khān</em> and <em>canis</em>. The mediaeval travellers latinized ‘Great Khāns’ by ‘Magni Canes’.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-204" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-204" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Robert Young.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-205" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-205" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">According to Valentyn, Abraham van Uffelen succeeded Andries Souri at Masulipatam in 1622, and remained chief till 1624.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-206" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-206" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Foot-post, courier.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-207" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-207" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Cf. ‘I have remov’d Falstaff’s horse, and he frets like a gumm’d velvet’ (<em>Henry IV (First Part)</em>, ii. 2).</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-208" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-208" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">A Turkish gold coin (Ibrāhīmī). Fryer (1677) mentions that in Persia the 'abrahmee' was worth twenty-seven or twenty-nine shāhīs (<em>9s.</em> or <em>9s. 8d.</em>).</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-209" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-209" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">‘The Mahometan priests are called Moolaas. ... These Moolaas ... employ much of their time as scriveners, to do business for others; or to teach their young children’ (<em>Terry's Voyage to East India</em>, ed. 1777, p. 247).</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-210" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-210" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">The caste of <em>Khatrīs</em> or weavers is still fairly strong in Surat and Ahmadābād (see <em>Bombay Gazetteer</em>, vol. ix. pt. 1, p. 188).</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-211" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-211" aria-label="Return to text">3.</a> <span class="fn-body">Gujarātī <em>Tagias</em>, a weaver caste doing better class work.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-212" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-212" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Hind. <em>shitranjī</em>, a coarse carpet of striped cotton.</span></li><li id="sec-07-fn-213" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-07-fn-213" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">i. e. ‘we cannot do without, if we are to lade this year’.</span></li></ol></section>
